Figure 8:
On-shell contributions to massive fermion scattering.

The general four fermion amplitude mediated by
s
-channel massive vector exchange is

\displaystyle A\left(\psi_{i},\psi_{j},\psi_{k},\psi_{l}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{s-m_{s_{M}}^{2}}\Big((t_{M})_{ij}(t_{M})_{kl}\left\langle\bf{13}\right\rangle\left[\bf{24}\bf\right]+(t_{M})_{ji}(t_{M})_{lk}\left[\bf{13}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{24}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-(t_{M})_{ij}(t_{M})_{lk}\left\langle\bf{14}\right\rangle\left[\bf{23}\bf\right]-(t_{M})_{ji}(t_{M})_{kl}\left[\bf{14}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{23}\right\rangle\Big)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{s-m_{s_{M}}^{2}}\frac{1}{2m_{s_{M}}^{2}}\Big((t_{M})_{ij}(t_{M})_{kl}\left(m_{1}\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]-m_{2}\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle\right)\left(m_{3}\left[\bf{34}\bf\right]-m_{4}\left\langle\bf{34}\right\rangle\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\,\,+(t_{M})_{ji}(t_{M})_{lk}\left(m_{2}\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]-m_{1}\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle\right)\left(m_{4}\left[\bf{34}\bf\right]-m_{3}\left\langle\bf{34}\right\rangle\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\,\,+(t_{M})_{ij}(t_{M})_{lk}\left(m_{1}\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]-m_{2}\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle\right)\left(m_{4}\left[\bf{34}\bf\right]-m_{3}\left\langle\bf{34}\right\rangle\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\,\,+(t_{M})_{ji}(t_{M})_{kl}\left(m_{2}\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]-m_{1}\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle\right)\left(m_{3}\left[\bf{34}\bf\right]-m_{4}\left\langle\bf{34}\right\rangle\right)\Big).

(94)

Here,
m_{i}
is the mass of external particle
i
and
m_{s_{M}}
denotes the mass of an exchanged particle labeled by species index
M
(of which there can be multiple). Repeated indices are implicitly summed over, even if they appear more than once. In the special case of a purely vectorial coupling (i.e. antisymmetric couplings), the amplitude simplifies to

\displaystyle A\left(\psi_{i},\psi_{j},\psi_{k},\psi_{l}\right)

\displaystyle=(t_{M})_{ij}(t_{M})_{kl}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(-\frac{\mathbf{\Pi}_{t}+\mathbf{\Pi}_{u}}{s-m_{s_{M}}^{2}}+\frac{(m_{1}-m_{2})(m_{3}-m_{4})(\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]+\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle)(\left[\bf{34}\bf\right]+\left\langle\bf{34}\right\rangle)}{2m_{s_{M}}^{2}(s-m_{s_{M}}^{2})}\right).

(95)

In the limit that
m_{1}=m_{2}
and
m_{3}=m_{4}
, this agrees with the standard QED amplitude (
83
) modified merely by shifting the pole(s) to
m_{s_{M}}^{2}
. In comparison, with a purely axial-vector coupling (symmetric coupling constants),

\displaystyle A\left(\psi_{i},\psi_{j},\psi_{k},\psi_{l}\right)

\displaystyle=(t_{M})_{ij}(t_{M})_{kl}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(-\frac{\mathbf{\Pi}_{t}-\mathbf{\Pi}_{u}}{s-m_{s_{M}}^{2}}+\frac{(m_{1}+m_{2})(m_{3}+m_{4})(\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]-\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle)(\left[\bf{34}\bf\right]-\left\langle\bf{34}\right\rangle)}{2m_{s_{M}}^{2}(s-m_{s_{M}}^{2})}\right).

(96)

While these processes are clearly already unitary in the HEL, it is amusing to add a contribution from a Higgs boson exchange in order to reconcile them with the simple result contained within the general
\mathcal{N}=2
superamplitude (
89
). A term proportional to
\mathbf{\Pi}_{s}
can be obtained by adding a pseudoscalar exchange amplitude with appropriate masses and couplings to the last term in (
3.4
):

\displaystyle A_{P}\left(\psi_{i},\psi_{j},\psi_{k},\psi_{l}\right)=-(t_{M})_{ij}(t_{M})_{kl}\frac{(m_{1}-m_{2})(m_{3}-m_{4})(\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]-\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle)(\left[\bf{34}\bf\right]-\left\langle\bf{34}\right\rangle)}{2m_{s_{M}}^{2}(s-m_{s_{M}}^{2})},

(97)

or similarly a scalar exchange to (
3.4
). The required mass and couplings of the pseudoscalar(s) should be implicitly obvious in (
97
) and will not be restated. The BPS condition on the masses then forces the overall coupling constant of the
\mathbf{\Pi}_{s}
term into agreement with the
\mathbf{\Pi}_{t}
and
\mathbf{\Pi}_{u}
terms. A general combination of scalars of either parity can be added to the general case (
3.4
) to reach a similar result.

In the HEL, the matching of (
3.4
) onto massless amplitudes is clear. The first set of terms each describe gluon exchange between different helicity configurations of fermions (in which the fermion pair on each side of the channel have opposite helicities). The remaining terms represent scalar exchanges that emerge as the longitudinal modes of the vectors. These have Yukawa couplings consistent with (
20
).

4
Massive Vector Boson Scattering, Gauged Non-linear Sigma Models and the Higgs Mechanism

4.1
General three vector boson amplitude

Before studying the three vector amplitude, it is first relevant and useful to establish the general amplitudes between two vector bosons and a scalar. These couplings have the general form and crossing properties:

\displaystyle A\left(g^{+}_{A},g^{+}_{B},\varphi_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{AB}^{i}}{\Lambda}\left[12\bf\right]^{2}

\displaystyle A\left(g^{-}_{A},g^{-}_{B},\varphi_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{(c^{i}_{AB})^{*}}{\Lambda}\left\langle 12\right\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle A\left(g^{+}_{A},W_{B},\varphi_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{AB}^{i}}{\Lambda}\left[1\bf{2}\bf\right]^{2}

\displaystyle A\left(g^{-}_{A},W_{B},\varphi_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{(c_{AB}^{i})^{*}}{\Lambda}\left\langle 1\bf{2}\right\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle A\left(W_{A},W_{B},\varphi_{i}\right)=\frac{c_{AB}^{i}}{\Lambda}\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]^{2}+\frac{(c_{AB}^{i})^{*}}{\Lambda}\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle^{2}+(-1)^{n_{1}+n_{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda^{i}_{AB}\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle.

(98)

The terms that are holomorphic in a particular spinor chirality correspond off-shell to dimension-
5
effective operators of the schematic form
\varphi F^{2}
(which I will mean to implicitly include the
\varphi F\widetilde{F}
counterpart as well). Bosonic exchange symmetry implies that
c_{AB}^{i}=c_{BA}^{i}
. The remaining coupling, special to the case of two massive vectors, can describe the interaction with Higgs bosons
9
provided that it is chosen to obey a particular constraint, as will be derived further below in Section
4.2
. This coupling is generally flavour symmetric
\lambda^{i}_{AB}=\lambda^{i}_{BA}
and real
(\lambda^{i}_{AB})^{*}=\lambda^{i}_{AB}
. I will generally refer to this as a Higgs coupling, and the associated scalar a Higgs boson, even if the further constraints demanded by the Higgs mechanism are not met.

The general
3
-particle amplitude for massive vector bosons decomposes into (mostly) independent Lorentz structures as

\displaystyle A(W_{A},W_{B},W_{C})=\frac{ih_{ABC}}{\Lambda^{2}}\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]\left[\bf{23}\bf\right]\left[\bf{31}\bf\right]+\frac{i\tilde{h}_{ABC}}{\Lambda^{2}}\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle\left\langle\bf{23}\right\rangle\left\langle\bf{31}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+(-1)^{n_{1}+n_{2}}\frac{i{f}_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C}}{2m_{1}m_{2}}\left[\bf{23}\bf\right]\left[\bf{13}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle+(-1)^{n_{1}+n_{2}}\frac{i{\tilde{f}}_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C}}{2m_{1}m_{2}}\left\langle\bf{23}\right\rangle\left\langle\bf{13}\right\rangle\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+(-1)^{n_{1}+n_{3}}\frac{i{f}_{CA}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,B}}{2m_{3}m_{1}}\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]\left[\bf{32}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{31}\right\rangle+(-1)^{n_{1}+n_{3}}\frac{i{\tilde{f}}_{CA}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,B}}{2m_{3}m_{1}}\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle\left\langle\bf{32}\right\rangle\left[\bf{31}\bf\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+(-1)^{n_{2}+n_{3}}\frac{i{f}_{BC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,A}}{2m_{2}m_{3}}\left[\bf{31}\bf\right]\left[\bf{21}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{23}\right\rangle+(-1)^{n_{2}+n_{3}}\frac{i{\tilde{f}}_{BC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,A}}{2m_{2}m_{3}}\left\langle\bf{31}\right\rangle\left\langle\bf{21}\right\rangle\left[\bf{23}\bf\right].

(99)

There is, however, one remaining redundancy among the terms with non-holomorphic spinor chiralities due to an identity derived by
68
:

\displaystyle(-1)^{n_{2}+n_{3}}m_{1}\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{23}\right\rangle\left[\bf{31}\bf\right]+(-1)^{n_{1}+n_{3}}m_{2}\left[\bf{23}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{31}\right\rangle\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]+(-1)^{n_{1}+n_{2}}m_{3}\left[\bf{31}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle\left[\bf{23}\bf\right]

\displaystyle-(-1)^{n_{2}+n_{3}}m_{1}\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle\left[\bf{23}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{31}\right\rangle-(-1)^{n_{1}+n_{3}}m_{2}\left\langle\bf{23}\right\rangle\left[\bf{31}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle-(-1)^{n_{1}+n_{2}}m_{3}\left\langle\bf{31}\right\rangle\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{23}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=0.

(100)

This redundancy can be accounted for in way preserving the identical particle exchange symmetry by introducing a shift invariance into the definitions of the
f
and
\tilde{f}
couplings:

\displaystyle{f}_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C}\mapsto{f}_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C}+a_{ABC}\qquad{f}_{BC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,A}\mapsto{f}_{BC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,A}+a_{ABC}\qquad{f}_{CA}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,B}\mapsto{f}_{CA}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,B}+a_{ABC}

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C}\mapsto\tilde{f}_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C}-a_{ABC}\qquad\tilde{f}_{BC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,A}\mapsto\tilde{f}_{BC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,A}-a_{ABC}\qquad\tilde{f}_{CA}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,B}\mapsto\tilde{f}_{CA}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,B}-a_{ABC}

(101)

for some collection of constants
a_{ABC}\in\mathbb{C}
that are fully antisymmetric under index exchanges. With self-conjugate states, unitarity relates the amplitude to its complex conjugate. Unitarity would therefore be manifest if the couplings were related so that
\tilde{h}_{ABC}=(h_{ABC})^{*}
and
\tilde{{f}}_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C}=({f}_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C})^{*}
. However, this latter equality may be spoiled by the shift redundancy in the definitions of these couplings. In order to preserve the appearance of manifest unitarity of the amplitude, it is therefore convenient to partially fix the redundancy so that the shift
a
is restricted to being purely imaginary.

In summary, the self-conjugate three vector amplitude can be written as:

\displaystyle A(W_{A},W_{B},W_{C})=\frac{ih_{ABC}}{\Lambda^{2}}\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]\left[\bf{23}\bf\right]\left[\bf{31}\bf\right]+\frac{i(h_{ABC})^{*}}{\Lambda^{2}}\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle\left\langle\bf{23}\right\rangle\left\langle\bf{31}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+(-1)^{n_{1}+n_{2}}\frac{i{f}_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C}}{2m_{1}m_{2}}\left[\bf{23}\bf\right]\left[\bf{13}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle+(-1)^{n_{1}+n_{2}}\frac{i({f}_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C})^{*}}{2m_{1}m_{2}}\left\langle\bf{23}\right\rangle\left\langle\bf{13}\right\rangle\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+(-1)^{n_{1}+n_{3}}\frac{i{f}_{CA}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,B}}{2m_{3}m_{1}}\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]\left[\bf{32}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{31}\right\rangle+(-1)^{n_{1}+n_{3}}\frac{i({f}_{CA}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,B})^{*}}{2m_{3}m_{1}}\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle\left\langle\bf{32}\right\rangle\left[\bf{31}\bf\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+(-1)^{n_{2}+n_{3}}\frac{i{f}_{BC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,A}}{2m_{2}m_{3}}\left[\bf{31}\bf\right]\left[\bf{21}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{23}\right\rangle+(-1)^{n_{2}+n_{3}}\frac{i({f}_{BC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,A})^{*}}{2m_{2}m_{3}}\left\langle\bf{31}\right\rangle\left\langle\bf{21}\right\rangle\left[\bf{23}\bf\right].

(102)

In this form, bosonic exchange symmetry clearly implies that the coupling constants are antisymmetric among exchanges of lowered indices, which, at this point, is the only significance of the index heights:

\displaystyle{f}_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C}=-{f}_{BA}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C}.

(103)

Furthermore, the couplings have the shift invariance

\displaystyle\Im f_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C}\mapsto\Im f_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C}+a_{ABC}\qquad\Im f_{BC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,A}\mapsto\Im f_{BC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,A}+a_{ABC}\qquad\Im f_{CA}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,B}\mapsto\Im f_{CA}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,B}+a_{ABC}

(104)

for any fully antisymmetric collection of constants
a_{ABC}\in\mathbb{R}
.

If the
f
-type couplings in (
4.1
) are all real and equal, then they can describe the coupling of three massive vector bosons in “spontaneously broken” YM
9
:

\displaystyle A\left(W_{A},W_{B},W_{C}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{if_{ABC}}{2m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}}\big((-1)^{n_{2}+n_{3}}m_{1}\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{23}\right\rangle\left[\bf{31}\bf\right]+(-1)^{n_{1}+n_{3}}m_{2}\left[\bf{23}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{31}\right\rangle\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+(-1)^{n_{1}+n_{2}}m_{3}\left[\bf{31}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle\left[\bf{23}\bf\right]+(-1)^{n_{2}+n_{3}}m_{1}\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle\left[\bf{23}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{31}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+(-1)^{n_{1}+n_{3}}m_{2}\left\langle\bf{23}\right\rangle\left[\bf{31}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle+(-1)^{n_{1}+n_{2}}m_{3}\left\langle\bf{31}\right\rangle\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{23}\right\rangle\big),

(105)

where
f_{ABC}={f}_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C}={f}_{BC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,A}={f}_{CA}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,B}
is real and fully antisymmetric. This expression is the kinematically unique structure contained in the
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM superamplitude (
75
) (in
109
, I explain more generally how the vector amplitude (
4.1
) embeds and is constrained in supersymmetric theories). This special case (
4.1
) is distinguished from all of its general deformations presented in (
4.1
) by the property that it has the softest possible HEL, scaling at worst as
\sim E
(which would qualify it as “tree unitary” if the standards of
56
were extrapolated to
3
-particle amplitudes with complex momenta). The high-energy dependence of the general amplitude will be elaborated upon further below - I will momentarily concentrate here on reviewing the limits specifically for (
4.1
).

In the HEL, the amplitude (
4.1
) converges as expected to the massless gluon amplitudes (
2.4.1
) when the vectors are all transversely polarised. The other nontrivial helicity configuration in this limit occurs when two of the vectors are longitudinal. In this case, it is easy to verify that the amplitude converges kinematically to that of massless scalars emitting a gluon in (
12
). Matching the coupling constants for the limit
A\left(W_{A},W_{B},W_{C}\right)\rightarrow A\left(\phi_{A},\phi_{B},g^{+}_{C}\right)
gives

\displaystyle(t_{C})_{AB}=\frac{-if_{ABC}}{2m_{1}m_{2}}\left(m_{1}^{2}+m_{2}^{2}-m_{3}^{2}\right).

(106)

This is the expected form of the Lie algebra generators describing the coupling of the emergent longitudinal scalar modes to gluons in the basis of mass eigenstates of the initial massive theory, as will be shown below. In this context, the labels
A
and
B
are to be reinterpreted as indices describing some components of the representation space of scalar states while otherwise being directly identified with the adjoint indices of the broken generators associated with the vectors.

Note that the explicit factors of particle masses in (
4.1
) have been chosen to ensure HEL convergence to the massless gluon amplitudes (
2.4.1
). Other choices lead to helicity configurations with either
\sim E/m
divergences (which would obstruct perturbative unitarity in the UV) or trivial three gluon amplitudes. This latter case amounts to “ungauging” the theory in the massless limit by having a mass-dependent gauge coupling. The mass dependence of the general expression (
4.1
) has been chosen to agree with (
4.1
) if full permutation antisymmetry is restored to the couplings. However, if the couplings to the different Lorentz structures in (
4.1
) are not equal, then there are helicity configurations involving longitudinally polarised modes that produce terms that diverge as
\sim E/m
and unitarily obstruct a consistent massless gluon amplitude from emerging. These divergent amplitudes have a UV form resembling those in (
98
) describing effective dimension-
5
interactions. This will be elaborated upon further below, but here it suffices to remark that these obstructions ensure that the only consistent massless gluon amplitudes that can emerge from (
4.1
) must have fully antisymmetric couplings, in agreement with the conclusions drawn in Section
2.4
about the inconsistency of other possibilities. Interestingly, in spite of this, the helicity configurations involving two longitudinal and one transverse polarisation have sensible limits for the general amplitude (
4.1
) regardless of the problems with the transverse configurations, assuming the explicit mass dependence as stated. In this general case, the gluon-scalar coupling that the amplitude matches onto is given by

\displaystyle(t_{C})_{AB}=\frac{-i}{2m_{1}m_{2}}\left(m_{1}^{2}\Re f_{BC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,A}+m_{2}^{2}\Re f_{CA}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,B}-m_{3}^{2}\Re f_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C}\right).

(107)

It will also be important to establish the HEL for the Higgs-like amplitude in (
98
). The coupling
\lambda
implicitly contains some inverse dependence on some mass scale. The leading high energy divergences of the amplitude occur when one of the vectors is longitudinal and the other is transverse. Matching the coupling constants from the limit
A\left(W_{A},W_{B},\varphi_{i}\right)\rightarrow A\left(g^{+}_{A},\phi_{B},\varphi_{i}\right)
gives

\displaystyle(t_{A})_{Bi}=\frac{im_{A}}{2}\lambda^{i}_{AB}.

(108)

In order to be consistent with the limit in which the helicity assignments are switched (like
A\left(W_{A},W_{B},\varphi_{i}\right)\rightarrow A\left(\phi_{A},g^{+}_{B},\varphi_{i}\right)
),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m_{A}}(t_{A})_{Bi}=\frac{1}{m_{B}}(t_{B})_{Ai}.

(109)

Again, the expected relation to Lie algebra representations will be derived in the Sections below.

I now return to the general amplitude between three massive vector bosons. In the limit that only one of the vectors is taken massless (choose here particle
3
), (
4.1
) becomes

\displaystyle A\left(W_{A},W_{B},g_{C}^{+}\right)

\displaystyle=i(-1)^{n_{1}+n_{3}}\,\Re f_{BC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,A}\frac{1}{mx}\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-i(-1)^{n_{1}+n_{2}}\left(f_{BC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,A}-f_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C}\right)\frac{1}{2m^{2}}\left[3\bf{2}\bf\right]\left[3\bf{1}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle

(110)

\displaystyle A\left(W_{A},W_{B},g_{C}^{-}\right)

\displaystyle=i(-1)^{n_{1}+n_{3}}\,\Re f_{BC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,A}\frac{x}{m}\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-i(-1)^{n_{1}+n_{2}}\left((f_{BC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,A})^{*}-(f_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C})^{*}\right)\frac{1}{2m^{2}}\left\langle 3\bf{2}\right\rangle\left\langle 3\bf{1}\right\rangle\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]

(111)

\displaystyle A\left(W_{A},W_{B},\phi_{C}\right)

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{2}(-1)^{n_{1}+n_{2}}\,\Re f_{BC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,A}\frac{\delta m}{m_{3}}\frac{1}{m}\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle

(112)

for each possible helicity configuration (I neglect the “holomorphic” terms because they are trivially affected by the limit and do not contain anything substantially new beyond the fully massive case). For this limit to work, the masses of the remaining massive vectors must converge to the same value at least as fast as
m_{3}\rightarrow 0
, so that
\delta m=(m_{2}-m_{1})=\mathcal{O}(m_{3})
(and
m
is the common mass that
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
converge to). The ratio
\delta m/m_{3}
is then some dimensionless coupling constant (possibly zero). Additionally, the couplings must also satisfy

\displaystyle f_{CB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,A}=-f_{CA}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,B},

(113)

which has been assumed in the expressions above. This is required for identical boson exchange symmetry, but does not completely enforce the full antisymmetry of the couplings that would be required for standard YM (
4.1
).

By “limit to work”, I mean that the transversely polarised amplitudes be Lorentz invariant (void of spurious spinors or “gauge artifacts” that may appear as path dependent choices of the limit), and the longitudinally polarised amplitude not diverge as
\sim 1/m_{3}
(which would otherwise provide a physical obstruction to taking the limit in a way consistent with perturbative unitarity). If the couplings are structure constants of a standard YM Lie algebra (and are therefore real and equal under cyclic permutations of indices at their default heights) then (
110
) reduces to minimal coupling. Deviations from this correspond to anomalous multipole moments of the form given by the second term in (
110
). See Appendix
C
for a simple example.

Finally, it should be noted that the limit of the amplitude with the longitudinal mode (
112
) has the form of the Higgs-like coupling in (
98
). This suggests that the longitudinal mode of the massive vector bosons could possibly play the role of a Higgs boson in unitarising vector boson scattering. Further comments on this will be made further below.

I conclude this subsection by providing an alternative representation of the three massive vector amplitude in which high-energy dependence is also made manifest:

\displaystyle A(W_{A},W_{B},W_{C})

\displaystyle=\frac{ih_{ABC}}{\Lambda^{2}}\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]\left[\bf{23}\bf\right]\left[\bf{31}\bf\right]+\frac{i(h_{ABC})^{*}}{\Lambda^{2}}\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle\left\langle\bf{23}\right\rangle\left\langle\bf{31}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{i\sqrt{2}}{3}\frac{c^{A}_{BC}}{\Lambda}\left(\frac{1}{m_{3}}\left[\bf{23}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{31}\right\rangle\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]-\frac{1}{m_{2}}\left[\bf{31}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle\left[\bf{23}\bf\right]\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{i\sqrt{2}}{3}\frac{(c^{A}_{BC})^{*}}{\Lambda}\left(\frac{1}{m_{3}}\left\langle\bf{23}\right\rangle\left[\bf{31}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle-\frac{1}{m_{2}}\left\langle\bf{31}\right\rangle\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{23}\right\rangle\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{i\sqrt{2}}{3}\frac{c^{B}_{CA}}{\Lambda}\left(\frac{1}{m_{1}}\left[\bf{31}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle\left[\bf{23}\bf\right]-\frac{1}{m_{3}}\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{23}\right\rangle\left[\bf{31}\bf\right]\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{i\sqrt{2}}{3}\frac{(c^{B}_{CA})^{*}}{\Lambda}\left(\frac{1}{m_{1}}\left\langle\bf{31}\right\rangle\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{23}\right\rangle-\frac{1}{m_{3}}\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle\left[\bf{23}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{31}\right\rangle\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{i\sqrt{2}}{3}\frac{c^{C}_{AB}}{\Lambda}\left(\frac{1}{m_{2}}\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{23}\right\rangle\left[\bf{31}\bf\right]-\frac{1}{m_{1}}\left[\bf{23}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{31}\right\rangle\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{i\sqrt{2}}{3}\frac{(c^{C}_{AB})^{*}}{\Lambda}\left(\frac{1}{m_{2}}\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle\left[\bf{23}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{31}\right\rangle-\frac{1}{m_{1}}\left\langle\bf{23}\right\rangle\left[\bf{31}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{if_{ABC}}{2m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}}\big(m_{1}\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{23}\right\rangle\left[\bf{31}\bf\right]+m_{2}\left[\bf{23}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{31}\right\rangle\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]+m_{3}\left[\bf{31}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle\left[\bf{23}\bf\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad+m_{1}\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle\left[\bf{23}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{31}\right\rangle+m_{2}\left\langle\bf{23}\right\rangle\left[\bf{31}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle+m_{3}\left\langle\bf{31}\right\rangle\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{23}\right\rangle\big).

(114)

I am not bothering to restate the crossing signs for brevity. The couplings introduced in (
4.1
) can be matched onto (
4.1
). Firstly, I define

\displaystyle f_{ABC}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}\left({f}_{BC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,A}+{f}_{CA}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,B}+{f}_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C}\right).

(115)

This is fully antisymmetric in its index exchanges. It is natural to fix the shift invariance (
104
) by setting
f_{ABC}
real and this has been done implicitly in (
4.1
). This coupling now corresponds directly to the spontaneously broken YM structure in (
4.1
) and isolates the terms with softened high energy dependence. However, further redundancy has been introduced elsewhere. The couplings of the form
c_{AB}^{C}
are complex, symmetric in their lowered indices (
c_{AB}^{C}=c_{BA}^{C}
) and obey the constraint

\displaystyle m_{1}c_{BC}^{A}+m_{2}c_{CA}^{B}+m_{3}c_{AB}^{C}=0.

(116)

They can be matched onto (
4.1
) through

\displaystyle\frac{c_{AB}^{C}}{\Lambda}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}m_{3}}\left({f}_{CA}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,B}-{f}_{BC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,A}\right)

\displaystyle\frac{c_{BC}^{A}}{\Lambda}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}m_{1}}\left({f}_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C}-{f}_{CA}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,B}\right)

\displaystyle\frac{c_{CA}^{B}}{\Lambda}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}m_{2}}\left({f}_{BC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,A}-{f}_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C}\right).

(117)

Alternatively,

\displaystyle{f}_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C}=f_{ABC}+\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{3}\left(m_{1}\frac{c^{A}_{BC}}{\Lambda}-m_{2}\frac{c^{B}_{CA}}{\Lambda}\right)

(118)

and similarly for the other permutations of indices. These equalities arise from direct comparison of each term in (
4.1
) with (
4.1
). Of course, these couplings are not linearly independent, so these equalities need only hold for a particular choice that are otherwise part of an equivalence class generated by (
104
) and (
116
). The matching presented above is nevertheless useful for making quick exchanges between the two descriptions (
4.1
) and (
4.1
) of the same amplitude.

The “holomorphic” terms in the first line of either (
4.1
) or (
4.1
) correspond off-shell to
F^{3}
and
F^{2}\widetilde{F}
operators (giving the real and imaginary parts of
h_{ABC}
). Their behaviour has a clear high energy dependence, scaling as
\sim E^{3}
in the HEL for transverse helicities all of the same sign and matching onto the massless amplitudes (
2.5
). The scaling of the remaining terms in the HEL is not so obvious.

Neglecting the
F^{3}
terms, there are two classes of terms contained in the remainder of the amplitude: those that scale as
\sim E^{2}
in the HEL and those that scale as the suppressed
\sim E
. Note that while this distinction is relevant to the present analysis, it does not automatically follow that the softer
3
-particle terms will generate softer higher leg amplitudes - this instead requires the further constraints on the couplings derived in the Sections below. The
\sim E
terms are isolated to the
f_{ABC}
term in (
4.1
) and its limits have been discussed above.

The class of terms proportional to the
c
-type constants in (
4.1
) have leading high-energy scaling
\sim E^{2}
for helicity configurations with two same-sign transverse vectors and one longitudinal vector. Assuming that the
c
-type constants do not contain inverse dependence on any of the particle masses, then, in the HEL, these terms match onto the corresponding massless amplitudes in (
98
) (hence the notational choices), while they vanish for other helicity configurations. I will denote by
(h_{1},h_{2},\dots)
a list of particle helicities describing a specific helicity configuration. To exemplify this matching, take the helicity configuration
(++,++,L)
(The
L
denotes longitudinal vector polarisation corresponding to helicity
0
or, equivalently,
(+-)
-valued spin indices), then the amplitude
A(W_{A},W_{B},W_{C})
converges to
A(g^{+}_{A},g^{+}_{B},\phi_{C})
and the coupling
c_{AB}^{C}
matches directly onto that in (
98
) with
C
reinterpreted as a “matter” index in a particular basis of scalar states. Through (
118
), this assumption about the (in)dependence of the
c
-type constants on the vector masses is tantamount to the assumption that the departure of the
f
-type couplings in (
4.1
) from full antisymmetry scales as
\mathcal{O}(\frac{m}{\Lambda})
, where
\Lambda
is some higher mass scale characterising the break-down of perturbative unitarity. However, if the
c
-type couplings are less mass suppressed, as suggested by (
4.1
), then they will obstruct the HEL of the subleading helicity configurations to which the YM terms would otherwise provide the unique, leading order contribution toward. This would altogether prevent a sensible HEL from existing and signify a theory with a low cut-off. Finally, I note that in the special case in which one of the vectors is massless (particle
3
, as above), then the constraint (
113
) translates into
c^{A}_{BC}=-c^{B}_{AC}
and the parameter
c^{C}_{AB}
disappears.

Off-shell, the real and imaginary parts of the non-holomorphic Lorentz structures encapsulated in each term of (
4.1
) correspond individually to operators (at leading order in the number of constituent fields) of the form
A_{\mu}A_{\nu}F^{\mu\nu}
and
A_{\mu}A_{\nu}\widetilde{F}^{\mu\nu}
. By themselves, in the high-energy limit, they converge at leading order to
\varphi FF
and
\varphi F\widetilde{F}
operators (from the Stuckelberg trick). However, the special gauge invariant linear combination that can be assembled into the non-kinetic parts of
F^{2}
corresponds to Yang-Mills theory and has the softened energy dependence. In this sense, the
c
-type couplings in (
4.1
) are interpreted as analogues of
\varphi F^{2}
dimension
5
operators in which the scalar is identified as the longitudinal mode of a massive vector boson. This is corroborated by the further results of this Section below.

4.2
Massive vector boson scattering and the Higgs mechanism

In contrast to massless
3
-particle amplitudes, massive amplitudes admit the possibility of multiple independent Lorentz structures, each of which may scale differently with high energies. The factorisable terms in
4
-leg amplitudes are likewise afflicted with this complication (in addition to the contact terms, for which this was obviously always an issue). The structures with the strongest energy dependence will eventually dominate the amplitude at high enough energies. At
4
-legs, as long as energy growing terms exist, perturbative unitarity will inevitably be violated at some point. In order to consistently extrapolate the theory to higher energies without obstruction from perturbative unitarity, these terms must be suppressed or eliminated in succession of their growth rates. This imposes a sequence of conditions on the underlying couplings of the theory required for the cancellation of the strongest to weakest growing terms. This sequence will be derived in the subsequent Sections. However, accepting some finite energy cut-off, the hierarchy in energy growth can generally be complicated by variations in the size of the associated coupling constants. Lorentz structures with stronger high-energy dependence can be suppressed by smaller couplings in order to make them comparable to terms with weaker energy dependence, provided that there is some energy ceiling. This allows for the possibility of a variety of low energy effective theories defined by different coupling hierarchies and which will be elaborated upon further below.

The “high energy limit” of a scattering amplitude is precisely defined here as
E\gg m
, where
E
stands generically for the energy of the external particles and
m
the particle masses, be they external or internal. Implicitly, this assumes the existence of a mass gap for the particles involved in each individual amplitude to which this is applied (I consider only scattering of mass eigenstates). Some more comments about this will be made below in Section
4.3.1
. If the theory violates perturbative unitarity in the HEL, I will use
\Lambda
to describe the scale at which this occurs, although it should be noted that this can depend upon the helicity sector under examination. If the theory admits an energy regime
\Lambda\gg E\gg m
, then it should match onto a sensible massless theory in the HEL, although this need not happen if
\Lambda
is not too much higher than the masses.

In the HEL, the factorisable terms in the amplitude fall into one of two possible categories: either they match onto massless amplitudes containing poles describing the exchange of a massless particle between two
3
-particle amplitudes, or they match onto
4
-particle contact terms. These contact terms always contain energy dependence of the form
(E/m)^{n}
(and multiplied by some combinations of coupling constants) for some
n\geq 0
, where
m
is the mass of an involved particle. If the contact terms involve scalars emerging from the longitudinal polarisations of the vector bosons, then it is possible that there is no corresponding contact term in the massive theory that would endow this emergent Lorentz structure with independent parametric freedom. In this case, the emergent
4
-particle contact coupling is entirely determined by the
3
-particle couplings of the massive amplitudes.

The massive terms matching onto massless exchange amplitudes can only be suppressed by suppressing the accompanying couplings constants. However, the emergent
4
-particle contact terms typically receive contributions from multiple factorisation channels and these can interfere with each other. The contact terms can therefore be suppressed by tuning the
3
-particle couplings to obey particular relations that ensures their cancellation. These relations usually resemble analogous constraints on the properties of massless
3
-particle couplings discussed above from consistency. This provides an alternative to suppressing the contact interactions independently of weakening the constituent
3
-particle couplings.

Massive Lorentz structures generally match onto a particular massless Lorentz structure at leading order in the HEL for a particular set of helicity configurations. However, they will also contribute spurious path-dependent spinors (or “gauge artifacts”) at subleading order in other helicity configurations. The existence of terms like this prevents the amplitude from being interpreted as matching onto a massless amplitude for these other helicity configurations. However, these limits are always obstructed by the leading divergences hitting the perturbative unitarity boundary. The unitarity bound and the appearance of Lorentz violating gauge artifacts are two separate manifestations of the same problem which is resolved by suppressing the couplings involved. This both extends the unitarity cut-off for the leading terms while relegating the spurious terms to ignorable subleading effects. Once these divergences have been suppressed or eliminated, the theory can be extrapolated to higher energies until it is obstructed again by the next strongest divergence in another helicity sector. This continues until all divergent terms have been eliminated and the theory is “unitarised”.

The goal of the present Section is to find the conditions under which, at tree-level,
2\rightarrow 2
vector boson scattering has the tamest high energy scaling. Doing so should uncover a sequence of conditions on the theory required to ameliorate the energy growth in distinct helicity sectors. These conditions delineate different classes of EFTs. The general
4
-leg massive vector scattering amplitude at tree-level decomposes into terms generated by two insertions of the
3
-leg amplitudes (
4.1
) and (
98
) and then, additionally,
4
-leg contact interactions. To identify the theory with the weakest HEL, the coefficients of the contact terms can be tuned to cancel divergences of the terms that correctly account for the factorisation properties. However, the lowest dimension contact interactions have mass dimension
4
(corresponding to dim-
8
operators) and, with one notable exception, cannot eliminate
\sim E^{3}
(or weaker) high-energy divergences in the amplitude’s factorisable sector. As will be shown below, this is inadequate for unitarising the amplitude, so further constraints on the
3
-particle amplitudes underpinning the factorising terms are required. This will lead to the emergence of Yang-Mills theory and the Higgs mechanism, but also a few other intriguing details.

It will be useful to introduce some new spinor structure building blocks. A basis of maximally symmetric, dimension
3
Lorentz structures consisting of a single spinor for each external particle is given by terms of the form

\displaystyle\mathbf{L}_{1}/m_{1}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\bf{1}\right\rvert p_{2}-p_{3}\left\lvert\bf{4}\bf\right]\left[\bf{23}\bf\right]+\left\langle\bf{1}\right\rvert p_{2}-p_{4}\left\lvert\bf{3}\bf\right]\left[\bf{24}\bf\right]+\left\langle\bf{1}\right\rvert p_{4}-p_{3}\left\lvert\bf{2}\bf\right]\left[\bf{43}\bf\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\langle\bf{1}\right\rvert p_{4}-p_{2}\left\lvert\bf{3}\bf\right]\left[\bf{42}\bf\right]+\left\langle\bf{1}\right\rvert p_{3}-p_{2}\left\lvert\bf{4}\bf\right]\left[\bf{32}\bf\right]+\left\langle\bf{1}\right\rvert p_{3}-p_{4}\left\lvert\bf{2}\bf\right]\left[\bf{34}\bf\right]

(119)

and similarly for
\mathbf{L}_{2}=\mathbf{L}_{1}|_{1\leftrightarrow 2}
,
\mathbf{L}_{3}=\mathbf{L}_{1}|_{1\leftrightarrow 3}
and
\mathbf{L}_{4}=\mathbf{L}_{1}|_{1\leftrightarrow 4}
. The structure
\mathbf{L}_{i}
is explicitly symmetric under exchanges of any pair of particles besides
i
. Parity conjugate structures
\hat{\mathbf{L}}_{i}
can be defined analogously with the bracket shapes switched. It will also be useful to define the following abbreviations:

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{s}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{34}\right\rangle\left[\bf{34}\bf\right]

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{t}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\bf{13}\right\rangle\left[\bf{13}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{24}\right\rangle\left[\bf{24}\bf\right]

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{u}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\bf{14}\right\rangle\left[\bf{14}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{23}\right\rangle\left[\bf{23}\bf\right].

(120)

Cancellations between terms generated in different factorisation channels can be manifested by the application of identities known as “syzygies”. These are relations in which linear combinations of spinor structures dressed by Mandelstam invariants are decomposed into linear combinations of lower dimensional Lorentz structures (with factors of masses accounting for the remaining dimensionality). Without the Mandelstam invariants, the spinor structures would otherwise be linearly independent and such a decomposition would not exist. These relations can be identified as massive deformations of simple massless identities. The simplest example deforms the trivial relation

\displaystyle\frac{\left\langle 34\right\rangle\left[43\bf\right]}{s}-\frac{\left\langle 31\right\rangle\left[13\bf\right]}{t}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow\frac{\left\langle\bf{3}\right\rvert p_{4}\left\lvert\bf{3}\bf\right]}{s-m_{s}^{2}}-\frac{\left\langle\bf{3}\right\rvert p_{1}\left\lvert\bf{3}\bf\right]}{t-m_{t}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{m_{3}\left(\left[\bf{3}\bf\right\rvert p_{1}p_{4}\left\lvert\bf{3}\bf\right]+\left\langle\bf{3}\right\rvert p_{1}p_{4}\left\lvert\bf{3}\right\rangle\right)}{(s-m_{s}^{2})(t-m_{t}^{2})}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{(m_{1}^{2}+m_{3}^{2}-m_{t}^{2})\left\langle\bf{3}\right\rvert p_{4}\left\lvert\bf{3}\bf\right]}{s-m_{s}^{2}}-\frac{(m_{3}^{2}+m_{4}^{2}-m_{s}^{2})\left\langle\bf{3}\right\rvert p_{1}\left\lvert\bf{3}\bf\right]}{t-m_{t}^{2}}.

(121)

As is clear from this example, these identities demonstrate the tension between manifest locality and high energy unitarity: one of these properties is obscured at the expense of the other. The cancellation between high energy dependence amalgamates terms from different factorisation channels.

Throughout the ensuing calculations, extensive use will be made of the syzygy representing the massive deformation of the all-channel pole:

\displaystyle\frac{\left[12\bf\right]\left[34\bf\right]}{s}+\frac{\left[13\bf\right]\left[24\bf\right]}{t}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow\frac{\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]\left[\bf{34}\bf\right]}{s-m_{s}^{2}}+\frac{\left[\bf{13}\bf\right]\left[\bf{24}\bf\right]}{t-m_{t}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{i}\mathbf{L}_{i}}{12(s-m_{s}^{2})(t-m_{t}^{2})}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{(\frac{1}{3}\sum_{i}m_{i}^{2}-m_{t}^{2})\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]\left[\bf{34}\bf\right]}{s-m_{s}^{2}}-\frac{(\frac{1}{3}\sum_{i}m_{i}^{2}-m_{s}^{2})\left[\bf{13}\bf\right]\left[\bf{24}\bf\right]}{t-m_{t}^{2}}.

(122)

This has been identified previously in a much less symmetric form in
68
. Related identities can be derived by contracting (
4.2
) with various combinations bilinears, which have application to amplitudes involving particles with various spins. In Appendix
A
, I present a list of relevant syzygies and other useful kinematic identities between Lorentz structures appearing in the
4
-particle amplitudes constructed in this study.

4.2.1
Partial unitarisation

For simplicity, I will ignore the
F^{3}
terms in the calculation presented in this Section, although their inclusion is straightforward and is done in Appendix
D.2
. Like their massless counterparts, double insertions of these produce terms that scale in the HEL as
\sim E^{4}
but cannot be canceled by contact interactions (this occurs, in particular, for the MHV helicity configuration). Searching for the theory with weakest high-energy scaling therefore immediately demands
h_{ABC}=0
. However, there nevertheless remains some further information that can be potentially extracted. Single insertions of
F^{3}
paired with the
f
-type terms in (
4.1
) produce
\sim E^{3}
high-energy divergences that can be reduced to
\sim E^{2}
(but no further) by demanding some sense of gauge covariance of the
h_{ABC}
couplings, as was demonstrated in the massless case in Section
2.5
. The complete analysis of this is provided in Appendix
D.2
, but does not affect the conclusions derived here.

Figure 9:
On-shell contributions to vector boson scattering (omitting
t
and

-channels).

Terms accounting for the factorisation structure of the amplitude can be constructed in the usual way by combining the
3
-leg amplitudes (
4.1
) across each channel on-shell. Making use of particle permutation symmetries and parity helps to economise the calculation, while keeping the intermediate particle’s momentum on-shell for as long as possible minimises the high-energy scaling. In the case at hand, the terms constructed from factorisation in this way have directly
\sim E^{3}
manifest high-energy scaling. As mentioned above, the contact terms for this amplitude have mass dimension
4
or greater, so (with one notable exception) scale as
\sim E^{4}
in the HEL at their weakest. Demanding the elimination of
\mathcal{O}(E^{4})
divergences therefore amounts to neglecting the separate inclusion of contact terms into the amplitude when the factorisation terms are constructed in this way. However, converting the expression to the
\{\mathbf{L}_{i}\}
basis (to manifest exchange symmetries) can reintroduce the need to tune the contact terms to cancel the divergences. Of utility in conversion to the basis of
\{\mathbf{L}_{i}\}
Lorentz structures are the identities

\displaystyle\left[\bf{1}\bf\right\rvert P_{s}\left\lvert\bf{3}\right\rangle\left[\bf{2}\bf\right\rvert P_{s}\left\lvert\bf{4}\right\rangle+\left[\bf{1}\bf\right\rvert P_{s}\left\lvert\bf{4}\right\rangle\left[\bf{2}\bf\right\rvert P_{s}\left\lvert\bf{3}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left(u-t\right)\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{34}\right\rangle+\frac{1}{6}\left(\frac{m_{2}}{m_{1}}\hat{\mathbf{L}}_{1}+\frac{m_{1}}{m_{2}}\hat{\mathbf{L}}_{2}+\frac{m_{4}}{m_{3}}\mathbf{L}_{3}+\frac{m_{3}}{m_{4}}\mathbf{L}_{4}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{2}{3}m_{1}m_{2}\left(\left\langle\bf{13}\right\rangle\left\langle\bf{24}\right\rangle+\left\langle\bf{14}\right\rangle\left\langle\bf{23}\right\rangle\right)+\frac{2}{3}m_{3}m_{4}\left(\left[\bf{13}\bf\right]\left[\bf{24}\bf\right]+\left[\bf{14}\bf\right]\left[\bf{23}\bf\right]\right)

(123)

and

\displaystyle\left\langle\bf{13}\right\rangle\left\langle\bf{2}\right\rvert P_{s}\left\lvert\bf{4}\bf\right]+\left\langle\bf{23}\right\rangle\left\langle\bf{1}\right\rvert P_{s}\left\lvert\bf{4}\bf\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{6m_{4}}\hat{\mathbf{L}}_{4}-\frac{1}{3}m_{4}\left(\left\langle\bf{14}\right\rangle\left\langle\bf{23}\right\rangle+\left\langle\bf{13}\right\rangle\left\langle\bf{24}\right\rangle\right)-m_{1}\left\langle\bf{23}\right\rangle\left[\bf{14}\bf\right]-m_{2}\left\langle\bf{13}\right\rangle\left[\bf{24}\bf\right],

(124)

where
P_{s}=p_{3}+p_{4}
is the intermediate
s
-channel on-shell momentum as defined in Section
2.2
above.

Regardless of the precise order of operations, an amplitude free of
\sim E^{4}
HEL divergences can be constructed that otherwise satisfies all factorisation requirements. The leading order
\sim E^{3}
terms occur for helicity configurations with single transverse and three longitudinal polarisations. For the helicity configuration
(L,L,L,++)
, the terms producing these divergences are, for the
s
-channel,

\displaystyle A_{E^{3}s}(W_{A},W_{B},W_{C},W_{D})

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{24\prod_{i}m_{i}}\frac{1}{s-m_{s_{M}}^{2}}\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]\left[\bf{34}\bf\right]\hat{\mathbf{L}}_{4}

\displaystyle\quad\times\left(\Re f_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\Re f_{CM}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D}+i\left(\Re f_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\Im f_{CM}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\Re f_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\Im f_{CD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}-\Im f_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\Re f_{CD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\right)\right)\right),

(125)

while
t
and
u
-channel terms are given by exchanging
2\leftrightarrow 3
and
1\leftrightarrow 3
respectively (including the corresponding internal indices). All other
\sim E^{3}
terms may be inferred from exchange symmetries and parity (implemented by switching the bracket shapes and complex conjugating the coupling constants).

Before presenting the remainder of the amplitude generated by factorisation, there remains some freedom among the contact interactions to be addressed. As alluded to above, there still exists a single independent contact interaction with
\sim E^{3}
high energy scaling
68
. Labeling

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}=\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{23}\right\rangle\left[\bf{34}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{41}\right\rangle-\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle\left[\bf{23}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{34}\right\rangle\left[\bf{41}\bf\right],

(126)

then the contact amplitude to be included is

\displaystyle A_{c}(W_{A},W_{B},W_{C},W_{D})=\frac{iC_{ABCD}}{\prod_{i}m_{i}}\mathbf{C},

(127)

which is parity violating. Here,
C_{ABCD}
are some coupling constants which must be fully antisymmetric in their indices in order to reflect the full antisymmetry of the accompanying kinematic factor under particle exchange (which is true but not manifest in the expression presented above). The choice of explicit mass dependence of the coefficient in (
127
) has been made purely for convenience. The leading divergences occur for helicity configurations with one transverse and three longitudinal polarisations, like for (
4.2.1
) above. The weaker HEL dependence than its mass dimension occurs because it is the difference of two linearly independent massive Lorentz structures that converge to the same massless Lorentz structure for the helicity configuration in which each massive spinor is unsuppressed (which is the 4
L
configuration). The contact amplitude (
127
) should be added to the terms constructed from factorisation in order to complete the full amplitude. Note that the kinematic factor is simply
\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\varepsilon_{1}^{\mu}\varepsilon_{2}^{\nu}\varepsilon_{3}^{\rho}\varepsilon_{4}^{\sigma}
, where
\varepsilon_{i}
are just the polarisations of the external vector bosons (see Appendix
C
).

Returning to the amplitude generated by factorisation, the remaining terms can be collected together into independent Lorentz structures identified by the helicity configurations for which they have the strongest high energy scaling. As for (
4.2.1
), I will present the
s
-channel expressions from which the complete
t
and
u
-channel expressions can be inferred from particle exchanges. For fully transverse helicities, the relevant terms are given by

\displaystyle A_{4TE^{2}s}(W_{A},W_{B},W_{C},W_{D})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{8m_{s_{M}}^{2}}\frac{1}{s-m_{s_{M}}^{2}}\left(\left(f_{MB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,A}+f_{MA}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,B}\right)\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]^{2}+\left((f_{MB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,A})^{*}+(f_{MA}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,B})^{*}\right)\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\left(\left(f_{MD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C}+f_{MC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D}\right)\left[\bf{34}\bf\right]^{2}+\left((f_{MD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C})^{*}+(f_{MC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D})^{*}\right)\left\langle\bf{34}\right\rangle^{2}\right).

(128)

The terms corresponding to two longitudinal polarisations and two opposite transverse helicities are given by (for
(--,L,L,++)
)

\displaystyle A_{2L2T\text{opp}E^{2}s}(W_{A},W_{B},W_{C},W_{D})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4m_{2}m_{3}}\frac{1}{s-m_{s_{M}}^{2}}\left(f_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}+(f_{BM}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,A})^{*}\right)\left((f_{CD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M})^{*}+f_{MC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D}\right)\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle\left\langle\bf{13}\right\rangle\left[\bf{24}\bf\right]\left[\bf{34}\bf\right]

(129)

plus all distinct terms generated particle exchanges (there are none in which both longitudinal modes appear on the same side of the channel). When the transverse helicities have the same sign, the terms are

\displaystyle A_{2L2T\text{same}E^{2}s}(W_{A},W_{B},W_{C},W_{D})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4m_{3}m_{4}m_{s_{M}}^{2}}\frac{1}{s-m_{s_{M}}^{2}}\left(f_{MB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,A}+f_{MA}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,B}\right)\left(m_{3}^{2}\Re f_{MD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C}+m_{4}^{2}\Re f_{MC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D}\right)\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]^{2}\left\langle\bf{34}\right\rangle\left[\bf{34}\bf\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{4m_{2}m_{4}}\frac{1}{s-m_{s_{M}}^{2}}\Big(f_{BM}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,A}f_{DM}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C}+i\Im f_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}f_{MD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C}+i\Im f_{CD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}f_{MB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,A}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\Re f_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\Re f_{CD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}-\Im f_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\Im f_{CD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\Big)\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]\left[\bf{13}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{24}\right\rangle\left[\bf{34}\bf\right]

(130)

for
(++,++,L,L)
and
(++,L,++,L)
, plus all distinct terms generated particle exchanges and parity conjugate terms. For fully longitudinal helicity configuration, the relevant terms are

\displaystyle A_{4LE^{2}s}(W_{A},W_{B},W_{C},W_{D})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\prod_{i}m_{i}}\frac{1}{s-m_{s_{M}}^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\bigg(\bigg(\frac{1}{3}\left(\Re f_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\left(m_{4}^{2}\Re f_{MC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D}-m_{3}^{2}\Re f_{MD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C}\right)+\Re f_{CD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\left(m_{2}^{2}\Re f_{MA}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,B}-m_{1}^{2}\Re f_{MB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,A}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+m_{s_{M}}^{2}\Re f_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\Re f_{CD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\bigg)\left(\mathbf{P}_{t}-\mathbf{P}_{u}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\frac{1}{m_{s_{M}}^{2}}\left(m_{1}^{2}\Re f_{MB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,A}+m_{2}^{2}\Re f_{MA}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,B}\right)\left(m_{3}^{2}\Re f_{MD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C}+m_{4}^{2}\Re f_{MC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D}\right)\mathbf{P}_{s}\bigg).

(131)

These are automatically
P
- and
T
-symmetric. The final class of terms is given by the expression

\displaystyle A_{3L1TEs}(W_{A},W_{B},W_{C},W_{D})

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{4\prod_{i}m_{i}}\frac{1}{s-m_{s_{M}}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\bigg(\bigg(\frac{1}{3}\left(m_{3}^{2}+m_{4}^{2}-m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}\right)\left(\Re f_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\Im f_{CD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}-\Re f_{CD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\Im f_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+m_{s_{M}}^{2}\left(\Re f_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\Im f_{CD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}+\Re f_{CD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\Im f_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\right)\bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad-\frac{2}{3}\Big(\Re f_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\left(m_{4}^{2}\Im f_{MC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D}-m_{3}^{2}\Im f_{MD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C}\right)+\Re f_{CD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\left(m_{2}^{2}\Re f_{MA}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,B}-m_{1}^{2}\Re f_{MB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,A}\right)\Big)\bigg)\mathbf{C}.

(132)

Being proportional to
\mathbf{C}
, this has weaker
\sim E
HEL scaling, which occurs for helicity configurations in which three of the polarisations are longitudinal and one is transverse. This class is
P
and
T
-violating.

Having now constructed a complete and correctly factorising amplitude, the next step is to identify the conditions under which it can be extrapolated to high energies with minimal obstruction from perturbative unitarity. Since the contact terms have been (mostly) fixed, demanding that the terms diverging as
\sim E^{3}
cancel necessitates constraints on the three vector self-couplings. Choosing, for example, the helicity configuration
(L,L,L,++)
, then the cancellation of the real (or
P
-conserving) part of the amplitude’s couplings (contained in (
4.2.1
)) across the three factorisation channels implies that

\displaystyle\Re f_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\Re f_{MC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D}+\Re f_{BC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\Re f_{MA}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D}+\Re f_{CA}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\Re f_{MB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D}=0.

(133)

This is the Jacobi identity. The real parts of the couplings must therefore be structure constants of a Lie algebra, as expected for Yang-Mills. However, unlike the massless case, there is no requirement that the Lie algebra be semi-simple or compact, even if the couplings are purely real. The coefficients of each of the Lorentz structures in (
4.1
) may differ. For the special case in which one of the vectors is a massless photon, departures from the standard properties materialise as large anomalous multipole moments of the charged massive vector bosons (see Appendix
C
). While the remaining
\sim E^{2}
HEL scaling of the amplitude is not unitary by the standards of renormalisable gauge theory, it is unitary by the standards of gravity, which explains the prevalence of non-semisimple or non-compact gauge algebras in theories of supergravity (see e.g.
110
;
70
;
108
- amplitudes for some theories with these features were also recently studied in
5d

46
).

Cancellation of the imaginary parts of the
\sim E^{3}
divergent couplings requires tuning the quartic (
127
) against the
P
-violating parts of (
4.2.1
) to fix:

\displaystyle C_{ABCD}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}\big(\Re f_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\Im f_{CD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}-\Re f_{CD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\Im f_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}+\Re f_{BC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\Im f_{AD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\Re f_{AD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\Im f_{BC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}-\Re f_{AC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\Im f_{BD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}+\Re f_{BD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\Im f_{AC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+2\left(\Re f_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\Im f_{CM}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D}-\Re f_{AC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\Im f_{BM}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D}+\Re f_{BC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\Im f_{AM}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D}\right)\big).

(134)

This is only consistent with the full antisymmetry of
C_{ABCD}
if

\displaystyle\Re f_{BC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\left(\Im f_{AD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}+\Im f_{AM}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D}\right)+\Re f_{BD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\left(\Im f_{AC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}+\Im f_{AM}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C}\right)+\Re f_{CA}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\left(\Im f_{BM}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D}+\Im f_{BD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\Re f_{DA}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\left(\Im f_{BM}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C}+\Im f_{BC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\right)+\Re f_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\left(\Im f_{CM}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D}+\Im f_{DM}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C}\right)=0.

(135)

Equivalently, this relation is required for the contact term to be sufficient to cancel the
P
-violating divergences for all
\sim E^{3}
helicity configurations. The constraint (
4.2.1
), in conjunction with (
103
) and (
104
), identifies the imaginary parts of the couplings off-shell as “generalised Chern-Simons terms”
60
;
6
, which are known to arise, for instance, in anomalous EFTs of massive vectors that can be UV completed to non-anomalous Yang-Mills. Deviations to the special quartic coupling (
127
) fixed by (
4.2.1
) may be regarded as “anomalous non-Abelian Generalised Chern-Simons quartic couplings” (aGCSQC), analogous to “anomalous quartic gauge couplings” described by different choices of
4
-leg contact interactions from spontaneously broken YM, except that these are distinguished as having slightly tamer high-energy scaling (“anomalous” in the sense of “irregular”, not in the sense of the chiral anomaly referred to in the previous sentence).

The Jacobi identity possesses a symmetrical structure in which each term clearly arises from the factorisation channel corresponding to the partitioning of the indices in the couplings. Each such term/channel is otherwise on identical footing as the others. Since the relation (
4.2.1
) has similar origins here, it is insightful to find a form with analogous symmetries. Defining
F_{AB;C}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D}=\Re f_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\Im f_{CM}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\Re f_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\Im f_{CD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}-\Re f_{CD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\Im f_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\right)
, then the GCS constraint becomes

\displaystyle F_{BC;A}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D}+F_{AB;C}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D}+F_{CA;B}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D}

\displaystyle\qquad+F_{DA;B}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C}+F_{AB;D}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C}+F_{BD;A}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C}=0,

(136)

which clearly has a similar
stu
-like and cyclic structure. The quartic coupling is also then

\displaystyle C_{ABCD}=\frac{1}{2}\left(F_{AB;C}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D}+F_{CA;B}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D}+F_{BC;A}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D}\right).

(137)

Having established (
133
), (
4.2.1
) and (
4.2.1
), the remaining amplitude now scales at worst as
\sim E^{2}
in the HEL. There are three sources of terms arising in the calculation at this point:

1.

terms constructed immediately from factorisation

2.

terms arising from cancellation between the GCS terms in (
4.2.1
) and the contact term (
127
)

3.

terms arising from cancellation between the pure Lie algebra terms in (
4.2.1
) across different channels.

The terms belonging to category
1
were constructed and presented above. The remaining
\sim E^{2}
terms require implementing the
\sim E^{3}
cancellations. To begin with, it is convenient to homogenise the masses appearing in the propagators in each channel. Selecting a single specific particle mass for each channel and denoting these simply as
m_{s}
,
m_{t}
and
m_{u}
(which are effectively free parameters), then e.g.

\displaystyle\frac{N_{m}}{s-m_{s_{m}}^{2}}=\frac{N_{m}}{s-m_{s}^{2}}+\frac{N_{m}\left(m_{s_{m}}^{2}-m_{s}^{2}\right)}{\left(s-m_{s}^{2}\right)\left(s-m_{s_{m}}^{2}\right)}.

(138)

Here
N_{m}
simply denotes the numerator associated to the
s
-channel exchange of the particles of mass
m_{s_{m}}
. I will refer to the terms proportional to
m_{s_{m}}^{2}-m_{s}^{2}
as the “mass splitting” terms. If
N_{m}\sim E^{4}
(or weaker) in the HEL, then these terms are clearly manifestly unitary and can therefore be left alone as they are. This will be the case in the examples covered in the subsequent Sections
4.3
and
4.4
below, where these terms will be acknowledged but mostly ignored (although they will play a role in matching the unitarised expressions onto their massless counterparts). However, in the case at hand, the numerator in (
4.2.1
) scales as
\sim E^{5}
, so they must be (temporarily) retained.

The mass eigenstate basis is the natural basis with which to describe the particle states involved in transition amplitudes. However, this may generally be disorganised and unnatural from the perspective of other internal quantum numbers. In this study, I do not intend to provide a detailed investigation into the question of representation reducibility and its physical manifestation as the phenomenon of mass mixing. This issue is secondary to establishing the algebraic structure of the couplings and has been the subject of
12
. However, the mass splitting terms invite the opportunity for some comments as they can describe finite mass mixing effects.

The four-fermion amplitude (
3.4
) in Section
3.4
provides a simple example with which this relation can be illustrated. For example, in the UV, the fermions could couple to the vector bosons (and therefore group into a non-trivial representation of the YM Lie algebra), but not have Yukawa couplings to the scalars (which may possibly be forbidden by symmetries again). However, in the IR, the vector-fermion coupling necessarily draws in the scalar identified as the longitudinal mode of the vector. There are two possible resolutions to make this consistent with vanishing UV Yukawa couplings. The first is that the coupling is vector-like and the fermions on each side of the channel have equal mass (as explained below (
20
)), so that the exchanged longitudinal mode directly decouples in the HEL. In the IR massive amplitude, this corresponds to the direct elimination of the
s
-wave term in (
3.4
), in which case the UV massless vector exchange amplitudes directly match onto the limits of the first term in (
3.4
). The second possibility is that additional scalar exchanges are introduced into the amplitude that cancel the UV effects of the longitudinal mode. These have Yukawa couplings determined from those required to cancel the
s
-wave terms in (
3.4
). However, the cancellation may not be complete against (
3.4
) in the IR because of the possible differences between masses of the scalars and the vectors in the propagators. The mass splitting terms, applied to this example, represent the residual mass mixing effects.

Returning to the four vector amplitude having homogenised the propagator masses, the
\sim E^{3}
cancellation between the terms with imaginary couplings occurs directly between the leading divergent terms in (
4.2.1
) and the contact term (
127
). This can be demonstrated by converting (
127
) to a form with manifest high energy dependence at the expense of manifest locality. Using (
4.2
), (
127
) can be expressed as e.g.

\displaystyle\frac{iC_{ABCD}}{\prod_{i}m_{i}}\mathbf{C}

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,=\frac{-1}{s-m_{s}^{2}}\frac{iC_{ABCD}}{\prod_{i}m_{i}}\Bigg(\frac{1}{12}\left(\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]\left[\bf{34}\bf\right]\sum_{j}\hat{\bf{L}}_{j}-\left\langle\bf{12}\right\rangle\left\langle\bf{34}\right\rangle\sum_{j}\mathbf{L}_{j}\right)+\left(m_{s}^{2}-\frac{1}{3}\sum_{j}m_{j}^{2}\right)\mathbf{C}\Bigg)

(139)

and there are analogous expressions with spurious
t
- and
u
-channel poles that may be easily arrived at by particle exchanges. The contact term (
127
) is most symmetrically represented as an average over all three such Mandelstam channel representations and I will assume this in the following. Hence (
4.2.1
) should be multiplied by
1/3
and added to the corresponding expressions with
1\leftrightarrow 3
and
2\leftrightarrow 3
. The HEL is now manifest and it is clear how the terms cancel against the leading energy terms in (
4.2.1
). The
s
,
t
and
u
-channel coupling partitions in (
4.2.1
), when substituted into (
4.2.1
), each cancel against the respective
s
,
t
and
u
-channel terms of the form (
4.2.1
). For example, the cancellation between the
s
-channel
\hat{\mathbf{L}}_{4}
term in (
4.2.1
) with the corresponding
s
-channel couplings in the
t
-channel representation of (
4.2.1
) leaves

\displaystyle A_{E^{2}st}^{(GCSb)}(W_{A},W_{B},W_{C},W_{D})

\displaystyle=\frac{-i}{72\prod_{i}m_{i}}\frac{1}{(s-m_{s}^{2})(t-m_{t}^{2})}\left(\Re f_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\Im f_{CM}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\Re f_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\Im f_{CD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}-\Im f_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\Re f_{CD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\hat{\mathbf{L}}_{4}\left(\frac{1}{12}\sum_{j}\mathbf{L}_{j}-\left(m_{s}^{2}-\frac{1}{3}\sum_{j}m_{j}^{2}\right)\left[\bf{13}\bf\right]\left[\bf{24}\bf\right]-\left(m_{t}^{2}-\frac{1}{3}\sum_{j}m_{j}^{2}\right)\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]\left[\bf{34}\bf\right]\right)

(140)

after application of (
4.2
). The corresponding cancellation with the
u
-channel representation of (
4.2.1
) is analogous and can be obtained from the above expression by exchanging
1\leftrightarrow 2
, while the cancellation with the
s
-channel representation is a direct elimination. Then the analogous argument can be applied to the cancellation of the
t
- and
u
-channel couplings. The left over terms
t
-coupling terms can be inferred from (
4.2.1
) by applying the exchanges
2\leftrightarrow 3
and also the sequence (
1\leftrightarrow 2
,
2\leftrightarrow 3
), while the
u
-coupling terms are obtained by adding both terms generated by the exchanges
1\leftrightarrow 3
and (
1\leftrightarrow 2
,
1\leftrightarrow 3
). This then completes the collection of residual
P
-violating terms that descend from the
\sim E^{3}
divergence in the
(L,L,L,++)
helicity configuration. The residual terms produced in the cancellation for the other helicity configurations are obtained in the same way as the other
E^{3}
terms generated from the action of exchanges and parity on (
4.2.1
). Along with the subleading terms proportional to
\mathbf{C}
on the right-hand side of (
4.2.1
), which I restate here for emphasis:

\displaystyle A_{Est}^{(GCSa)}(W_{A},W_{B},W_{C},W_{D})

\displaystyle=\frac{-i}{3\prod_{i}m_{i}}C_{ABCD}\mathbf{C}\Bigg(\frac{m_{s}^{2}-\frac{1}{3}\sum_{j}m_{j}^{2}}{s-m_{s}^{2}}+\frac{m_{t}^{2}-\frac{1}{3}\sum_{j}m_{j}^{2}}{t-m_{t}^{2}}+\frac{m_{u}^{2}-\frac{1}{3}\sum_{j}m_{j}^{2}}{u-m_{u}^{2}}\Bigg),

(141)

these constitute the category 2 terms descending from the reduction of the GCS divergences.

Finally are the terms in category 3: the
\sim E^{2}
terms that arise from reducing the
P
-conserving
\sim E^{3}
terms in (
4.2.1
). Generally, it is useful to write the amplitude in the form

\displaystyle\frac{C_{s}N_{s}}{s-m_{s}^{2}}+\frac{C_{t}N_{t}}{t-m_{t}^{2}}+\frac{C_{u}N_{u}}{u-m_{u}^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad=\frac{1}{3}\Bigg(\left(C_{s}-C_{t}\right)\left(\frac{N_{s}}{s-m_{s}^{2}}-\frac{N_{t}}{t-m_{t}^{2}}\right)+\left(C_{t}-C_{u}\right)\left(\frac{N_{t}}{t-m_{t}^{2}}-\frac{N_{u}}{u-m_{u}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+\left(C_{u}-C_{s}\right)\left(\frac{N_{u}}{u-m_{u}^{2}}-\frac{N_{s}}{s-m_{s}^{2}}\right)\Bigg)

(142)

where
C_{x}
are combinations of coupling constants for channel
x
obeying
C_{s}+C_{t}+C_{u}=0
and
N_{x}
are the corresponding kinematical factors. If the pairings of kinematical terms in (
4.2.1
) can be combined to produce expressions with manifestly reduced high energy scaling, then (
4.2.1
) provides a symmetrical way of accounting for this.

Applying (
4.2.1
) to the
\hat{\mathbf{L}}_{4}
terms generated by (
4.2.1
) gives

\displaystyle A^{(J)}_{E^{2}st}(W_{A},W_{B},W_{C},W_{D})

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{72\prod_{i}m_{i}}\left(\Re f_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\Re f_{MC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D}-\Re f_{CA}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\Re f_{MB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D}\right)\left(\frac{\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]\left[\bf{34}\bf\right]}{s-m_{s}^{2}}+\frac{\left[\bf{13}\bf\right]\left[\bf{24}\bf\right]}{t-m_{t}^{2}}\right)\hat{\bf{L}}_{4}

(143)

for the
s
and
t
-channel pairings. The terms in (
4.2.1
) grouping the
t
and
u
channels are given by the exchange
1\leftrightarrow 3
, while the
s
and
u
grouping is given by
1\leftrightarrow 2
. The kinematic factors can be reduced using (
4.2
) to give

\displaystyle A^{(J)}_{E^{2}st}(W_{A},W_{B},W_{C},W_{D})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{72\prod_{i}m_{i}}\frac{1}{(s-m_{s}^{2})(t-m_{t}^{2})}\left(\Re f_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\Re f_{MC}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D}-\Re f_{CA}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\Re f_{MB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\hat{\bf{L}}_{4}\left(\frac{1}{12}\sum_{j}\mathbf{L}_{j}-\left(m_{s}^{2}-\frac{1}{3}\sum_{j}m_{j}^{2}\right)\left[\bf{13}\bf\right]\left[\bf{24}\bf\right]-\left(m_{t}^{2}-\frac{1}{3}\sum_{j}m_{j}^{2}\right)\left[\bf{12}\bf\right]\left[\bf{34}\bf\right]\right),

(144)

which now has manifest
\sim E^{2}
HEL scaling. As described with (
4.2.1
), to this class of terms generated by
A^{(J)}_{E^{2}st}(W_{A},W_{B},W_{C},W_{D})
and its symmetrisation of the form (
4.2.1
) should be added the parity conjugate terms (obtained by swapping the bracket shapes) and the terms exchanging particle
4
with the each of the others. This completes the remaining class of terms in the partially unitarised amplitude.

The structures
\hat{\mathbf{L}}_{4}\mathbf{L}_{j}
containing the leading energy dependence in (
4.2.1
) and (
4.2.1
) can be decomposed into a basis of lower dimensional spinor structures multiplied by Mandelstam invariants and masses in (
A
) and (
A
). The leading order
\sim E^{2}
terms all have only a single pole and can be combined with the terms constructed from factorisation that are stated above. This will be pursued in the next subsection in the course of identifying the conditions required for further unitarisation.

In the special case in which some of the external vector bosons are massless, some of the conditions represented in (
133
) and (
4.2.1
) become mandatory consequences of consistent factorisation rather than features of
high-energy unitarity. This can be demonstrated directly and explicitly by using the systematic soft limit procedure formulated by
72
, which was subsequently used by
76
for massive amplitudes. I review and utilise this in
109
and will not recapitulate it here, but instead just sketch out the argument and the result. In short however, when applied to a
4
-particle amplitude, this procedure is simply an automatisation of the
4
-particle test from which the consistency conditions can be directly extracted. Complex factorisation is triggered by taking a massless gluon leg soft in a spinorially holomorphic way. The gluon coupling to the massive vectors is given by (
110
), but only the minimal coupling term is in tension with factorisation. Taking an
4
-leg amplitude with one external gluon and three external massive vectors, then when the minimal gluon coupling (
110
) and (
4.1
) are combined across factorisation channels, the resulting consistency constraints become

\displaystyle\Re f_{AB}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}\big(

\displaystyle(f_{MD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,A})^{*}m_{1}\left\langle\bf{41}\right\rangle\left\langle\bf{31}\right\rangle\left[\bf{34}\bf\right]+(f_{AM}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D})^{*}m_{4}\left\langle\bf{34}\right\rangle\left\langle\bf{14}\right\rangle\left[\bf{13}\bf\right]+(f_{DA}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M})^{*}m_{3}\left\langle\bf{13}\right\rangle\left\langle\bf{43}\right\rangle\left[\bf{41}\bf\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+f_{MD}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,A}m_{1}\left[\bf{41}\bf\right]\left[\bf{31}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{34}\right\rangle+f_{AM}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D}m_{4}\left[\bf{34}\bf\right]\left[\bf{14}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{13}\right\rangle+f_{DA}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,M}m_{3}\left[\bf{13}\bf\right]\left[\bf{43}\bf\right]\left\langle\bf{41}\right\rangle\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\left(1\leftrightarrow 3\right)+\left(3\leftrightarrow 4\right)=0,

(145)

where particle
2
(colour
B
) has been chosen as the massless gluon and (
113
) has been used (it is also possible to construct an analogous argument when a second vector is also massless using (
110
) again instead of (
4.1
)). The multiple independent Lorentz structures lead to multiple consistency conditions that can be read off once the redundancy (
4.1
) is fixed in some way. This reproduces the subset of Jacobi relations (
133
) and non-Abelian GCS constraints (
4.2.1
) in which the massless colour index is exclusively lowered. Because of (
113
), the general non-Abelian GCS constraint equations (
4.2.1
) collapse from ten to six terms when colour
B
is taken massless (one pair emerging from each of the three factorisation channels), which reconciles it with the constraints enforced here.

The non-Abelian GCS constraints are a notable counterexample to the naive expectation that the couplings between massive particles should be covariant tensors of the Lie algebra. Even when some of the vectors are massless gluons, the consistency requirement from the soft gluon limit does not produce a covariance relation. This is possible because the Lorentz structures corresponding to each class of coupling constants do not remain linearly independent across factorisation channels. The resulting consistency conditions arising from single soft gluon limits can therefore mix couplings across different structures. This is in contrast to e.g. the Yukawa coupling amplitudes (
16
), which do not kinematically change when a soft gluon is emitted off each leg.

At this point, it is simple to include the possible contribution to the amplitude from the tree-level exchange of scalars with the couplings given in (
98
). The
s
-channel exchange amplitude is given by
